Define a total order per dimension in greenkube.models.provenance (cost: opencost > opencost_derived > list_price > custom_rate > none; requests/owner: observed > unknown; usage: prometheus container series > pod series > metrics API). The job must load the stored rows of the window, and for each key keep the stored value of a dimension when the incoming provenance is strictly worse (cost fields together with cost_source; cpu_request, memory_request, cpu_limit, memory_limit, owner_kind, owner_name together). Rows the new pass did not return are kept only if step 2's delete is restricted to rows whose every dimension is not better than the new pass; simplest rule: when the new pass is degraded on any cluster-wide dimension (OpenCost unavailable, pod list failed, pods of the window no longer in the API), do not replace the window at all, log it and export `greenkube_collection_recollect_skipped_total{reason}`. 4. Energy, CO2e and modelled cost are shares of a node total: they must be recomputed for the whole window from the merged inputs (never mixed row by row). 5. Backfill: none possible for already degraded rows; document in CHANGELOG. Expose `collected_at` in the metrics API.

**Acceptance.** a) Collect window W with OpenCost and live pods; delete pod P; re-collect W with `--last` while OpenCost is down: P's row keeps cost_source=opencost, its cost, cpu_request>0 and owner; the test of SIM B prints the first-pass rows unchanged. b) Re-collect W with a strictly better pass (OpenCost back after an outage): costs are replaced and cost_source=opencost. c) After any re-collection, all rows of W have the same collection_run_id and SUM(joules) of pod rows + idle rows equals the node energy of the model for W (within 1e-6). d) Same tests on PostgreSQL and SQLite.

#### STO-02 · Hourly compression is not bounded by the collection watermark in production (the provider is never passed), so hours are closed before their rows are collected and the late rows are never aggregated.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** bug · **Confidence** confirmed-sim · **Effort** M

**Where.** `src/greenkube/cli/start.py:251`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:10`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:328-340`; `src/greenkube/demo/runner.py:180`

**Mechanism.** The module docstring of metrics_compressor.py states that only hours `<= floor_hour(min(now - METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS, collection_watermark))` are compressed, and `_compression_limit` implements it through `collection_watermark_provider`. The scheduled job builds `MetricsCompressor(get_db_manager())` without that argument (only tests/core/test_metrics_compressor_lossless.py:248 passes it), so the limit is `now - age` alone. When collection is behind that limit (collection stalled on a failing window, COLLECTION_MAX_BACKFILL larger than METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS, METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS lowered, or `greenkube start --last` older than the age), the compressor aggregates the hour from the rows present (possibly none), advances `metrics_compression_watermarks`, and never re-reads the hour (`_resume_point` starts at the watermark). Rows collected afterwards stay raw, are ignored by every read that uses the hourly table before the cutoff, and are deleted at METRICS_RAW_RETENTION_DAYS.

**Effect on numbers.** All energy, CO2e, cost and usage of the late hours are missing from history older than METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS (dashboard, reports, yearly totals) and from long-window rightsizing. Simulation: two hours of 0.12 each were stored raw; after compression the hourly table holds one hour and aggregate_summary returns total_cost 0.12 instead of 0.24 (-50%), energy 1200 J instead of 2400 J.

**Affected environments.** All databases. Default settings are exposed only through `--last` ranges older than 24 h and through a compression run during a long catch-up; any install with COLLECTION_MAX_BACKFILL > 24h or METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS < backfill is exposed after each outage.

**Evidence.** Simulation audit-workspace/audit/work/storage-write/sim_compress.py (real MetricsCompressor built as in cli/start.py, real SQLite repositories): collection watermark 09:00, compression watermark moved to 10:00; after the 09:00 hour was collected a second compression run compressed 0 rows; hourly table has only 08:00; aggregate_summary total_cost=0.12, row_count=1. Code: grep shows no production caller passing collection_watermark_provider.

**Fix.** 1. In cli/start.py `compress_metrics`, build the compressor with `collection_watermark_provider=lambda: watermark_repo.get(cluster_uid, METRICS_WATERMARK_SOURCE)` returning `last_complete_end` (use the same cluster_uid resolution as MetricsCollectionJob; when no watermark row exists, return the epoch so nothing is compressed before the first complete window). Do the same in demo/runner.py or pass an explicit `None` with a comment. 2. Make the argument mandatory (keyword-only without default) so that a new caller cannot forget it. 3. Repair path for rows already stranded and for forced `--last` ranges: add `MetricsCompressor.recompress(start, end)` that re-aggregates given closed hours from the raw rows still present, and call it from MetricsCollectionJob whenever a window with `window.start < compression_watermark` is written (same transaction as the rows). At startup run it once for every hour before the compression watermark that still has raw rows whose count differs from `combined_metrics_hourly.sample_count` (one-off repair, logged). 4. Export `greenkube_compression_lag_hours` and a warning when the collection watermark is older than `now - METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS`.

**Acceptance.** sim_compress.py scenario as a test: with the collection watermark at 09:00, a run at D+1 10:30 leaves the compression watermark at 09:00; after the 09:00 hour is collected and the watermark advanced, the next run produces the 09:00 hourly row with sample_count=12 and aggregate_summary returns 0.24. A forced `--last` window inside an already compressed hour updates that hourly row (sample_count and sums equal to the raw rows).

#### STO-03 · A collection window is committed in four independent transactions (pod rows, container rows, idle rows, watermark) and PostgreSQL node snapshots in one autocommit statement per step, so a failure or a concurrent reader sees a partial window.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** architecture · **Confidence** confirmed-code · **Effort** M

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/collection.py:316-334`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:235`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:375-377`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:552-553`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:575-576`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/collection_watermark_repository.py:1-60`; `src/greenkube/storage/unit_of_work.py:1-86`

**Mechanism.** `MetricsCollectionJob._collect_window` calls `write_combined_metrics(rows)` (which commits the pod rows, then opens a second connection and commits the container rows), then `write_idle_metrics` (third commit), then `watermark_repository.advance` (fourth commit). Each uses `db_manager.connection_scope()`; `UnitOfWork` exists but is not used on this path. Consequences: (1) readers (API, recommendation engine, change measurement, the hourly compressor) can read a step with pod rows but no container rows or no `__idle__` rows; (2) when the container or idle write fails, the pod rows of the window stay while the watermark does not move; if the failure persists (for example one bad container row raises ValueError at postgres/repository.py:519 for the whole batch) the job re-collects the same window on every run and never reaches the following ones (head-of-line blocking), and after COLLECTION_MAX_BACKFILL the window is abandoned with its partial content; (3) a forced `--last` pass that is not contiguous with the watermark has no completion marker at all. Nothing records that a stored window is partial.

**Effect on numbers.** For a partial window, pod totals are present but node totals (pods + idle) miss the idle share and container-level rightsizing evidence is missing for that hour; with 5-minute steps and one-hour chunks one failed idle write removes 12 steps of idle energy and cost per node. During every normal write there is a short interval where a dashboard query returns pod rows of the newest step without the idle and container rows.

**Affected environments.** All; PostgreSQL and SQLite.

**Evidence.** Code path read end to end (collection.py:316-334; each repository method opens its own connection_scope and commits). SIM A2 in sim_storage.py shows the watermark advancing after a partially failed write on SQLite.

**Fix.** 1. Add `write_window(window, pod_rows, container_rows, idle_rows, cluster_uid, source)` to the metrics repositories: one `UnitOfWork` (PostgreSQL: one connection + transaction; SQLite: BEGIN IMMEDIATE) that upserts the three row sets and advances the watermark with the same connection, then commits. The watermark repository gets an `advance(..., connection=...)` variant. 2. MetricsCollectionJob calls only `write_window`; leader-fence check immediately before it. 3. Validate rows before the transaction and quarantine invalid ones with a counter (`greenkube_collection_rows_rejected_total{reason}`) and an entry in a new `collection_window_log` table (window start/end, run id, rows per table, rejected rows, status complete|partial|failed) instead of failing the whole batch forever; the API health payload exposes the last partial or failed windows. 4. PostgreSQL batches: keep one transaction but send rows in chunks of at most 5,000 per executemany. 5. Same transaction rule for PostgresNodeRepository.save_nodes (see STO-06).

**Acceptance.** Fault-injection test on both dialects: make write of idle rows raise; afterwards the window has 0 pod rows, 0 container rows and the watermark is unchanged. Make it succeed: all three sets and the watermark appear atomically (a concurrent reader polling during the write never observes pod rows of a step without its idle rows). A row with a NULL container timestamp is rejected and counted, the other rows of the window are stored and the window is logged as partial.

#### STO-04 · The SQLite metrics writer swallows per-row errors and still commits, so the window is reported written and the watermark advances over missing rows.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** bug · **Confidence** confirmed-sim · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/repository.py:302-426`; `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/repository.py:421-424`; `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/repository.py:261-265`

**Mechanism.** `SQLiteCombinedMetricsRepository.write_combined_metrics` wraps each row INSERT in `try: ... except sqlite3.Error: logging.error(...) except Exception: logging.error(...)` and then commits the rows that succeeded. The method returns normally, so `MetricsCollectionJob` counts the window as collected and advances the watermark. The PostgreSQL writer is all-or-nothing (executemany raises), so the two dialects behave differently, and every test that runs on SQLite cannot detect a rejected row. `save_history` of the carbon-intensity repository has the same per-row swallow.

**Effect on numbers.** Each swallowed row removes one pod-step of energy, CO2e and cost permanently (the window is never re-collected). Simulation: 3 rows produced, 2 stored, report.error=None, watermark advanced to the end of the window; with a transient `database is locked` (another process holding the write lock for 7 s) the first row was lost the same way.

**Affected environments.** SQLite only (`greenkube demo`, local development, CLI use, and the whole SQLite test suite). Not the Helm install (PostgreSQL only).

**Evidence.** Simulation audit-workspace/audit/work/storage-write/sim_storage.py SIM A1 (value out of range) and SIM A2 (transient lock): `report: collected=1 rows_written=2 error=None watermark=2026-11-15 10:05:00+00:00`, stored rows api-1/api-3 (A1) and api-2/api-3 (A2).

**Fix.** Remove the inner try/except in write_combined_metrics (and in SQLiteCarbonIntensityRepository.save_history): run all INSERTs of the batch in one explicit transaction (`db_manager.transaction(immediate=True)`), let any error roll the batch back and raise QueryError, exactly like the PostgreSQL writer. Set `PRAGMA busy_timeout` explicitly (for example 30000 ms) in DatabaseManager.setup_sqlite. Return the number of rows given, not the sum of cursor.rowcount.

**Acceptance.** SIM A1 and A2 as tests: write_combined_metrics raises QueryError, no row of the batch is stored, MetricsCollectionJob.run returns error != None and the watermark is unchanged; the next run stores the 3 rows.

#### STO-05 · combined_metrics.id and combined_metrics_hourly.id are 32-bit SERIAL and every upsert consumes a sequence value even when it updates, so large or long-lived installs stop collecting when the sequence is exhausted.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** reliability · **Confidence** confirmed-live · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0000_initial_schema.sql:3-4`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0002_hourly_aggregation_and_node_scd2.sql:9-10`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0002_hourly_aggregation_and_node_scd2.sql:62`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:236-274`

**Mechanism.** Live schema: combined_metrics.id integer, combined_metrics_hourly.id integer, node_snapshots_scd.id integer, carbon_intensity_history.id integer (container_metrics, node_idle_metrics are bigint). `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` calls nextval() for every proposed row, including the rows that end as an UPDATE (re-collections, the hourly compression upsert, the 24 h carbon recompute does not, but every `--last` and every watermark fork does). At 2,147,483,647 PostgreSQL raises `nextval: reached maximum value of sequence`, every write of the batch fails, the watermark stops, and after COLLECTION_MAX_BACKFILL the missed windows are abandoned.

**Effect on numbers.** Rows per day = pods x 288 at the default 5-minute step. 2,000 pods: 576,000 ids/day, exhaustion after 10.2 years; 20,000 pods: 1.0 year; 5,000 pods with a 1-minute step: 298 days. After that, no metric is stored at all (100% loss) until an operator alters the column. node_snapshots_scd currently burns 2 ids per node per cycle (NRG-07): 708 versions per node in 12.5 h on kind.

**Affected environments.** PostgreSQL (the only database of the Helm install). Large clusters, short steps or long operation.

**Evidence.** Live: information_schema shows integer(32) for the ids; on kind `select count(*), max(id) from combined_metrics` = 26424 / 27277 (853 values consumed by upserts that updated).

**Fix.** New migration (PostgreSQL): `ALTER SEQUENCE ... AS BIGINT` and `ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN id TYPE BIGINT` for combined_metrics, combined_metrics_hourly, node_snapshots_scd, node_snapshots, carbon_intensity_history and every other table whose id is int4 (list them from information_schema). The column rewrite takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock and rewrites the table: document the duration in docs/upgrading.md, or for large tables do it online (add id_new BIGINT, backfill in batches, swap). Better: drop the surrogate id from the raw and hourly metric tables and make the natural key the primary key (the id is only used by the carbon recompute join, which can use ctid-free key joins). Add a startup check that warns when any sequence is above 50% of its type.

**Acceptance.** After the migration `select data_type from information_schema.columns where column_name='id' and table_name in (...)` returns bigint everywhere; a test sets the sequence to 2,147,483,640, writes 20 rows and succeeds; tests/integration/test_upgrade_from_v031.py still passes.

#### STO-06 · Node snapshot versions (SCD2) are compared against float4 columns and written without a transaction or a uniqueness constraint, so any non-float4-exact capacity or embodied value creates a new version on every cycle and two writers can leave zero or two current rows.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** data-integrity · **Confidence** confirmed-code · **Effort** M

**Where.** `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/node_repository.py:22-33`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/node_repository.py:50-115`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0002_hourly_aggregation_and_node_scd2.sql:62-77`; `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/node_repository.py:128`

**Mechanism.** `node_snapshots_scd.cpu_capacity_cores` and `embodied_emissions_kg` are REAL (float4) in PostgreSQL. `save_nodes` reads the current row and compares `current[col] != getattr(node, col)` in Python. asyncpg returns the float4 widened to a double, so a stored 1037.3 comes back as 1037.300048828125 and never equals the collector's 1037.3: the row is closed and a new version inserted on every call. Today the values are 14 cores and the flat 100 kg default (NRG-01), which are float4-exact, so the churn seen live (708 versions per node in 12.5 h) is the NULL/100 flip of NRG-07; as soon as real Boavizta values or fractional allocatable CPU are stored, the float4 mismatch produces the same churn by itself. Separately: the close (UPDATE ... is_current=FALSE) and the INSERT are two autocommit statements on a plain `connection_scope()` (no transaction), there is no unique index on (node_name) WHERE is_current, and two writers (node analysis and the embodied service) call save_nodes within seconds of each other. A crash or an interleaving between the two statements leaves a node with no current row or with two. `get_latest_snapshots_before` then returns an arbitrary one (ORDER BY valid_from DESC, first seen). The PostgreSQL fallback timestamp is `datetime.utcnow()` (naive). The SQLite repository additionally inserts one legacy `node_snapshots` row per node per call; neither that table nor node_snapshots_scd is pruned by the retention job (metrics_compressor._prune_raw covers only the metric tables).

**Effect on numbers.** Unbounded growth: 2 versions per node per cycle (about 5,760 rows per node per day at a 30 s cycle, 576 at 5 minutes) slows every snapshot read and consumes the int4 id (STO-05). With two current rows for a node, historical reconstruction (RECON-05) can pick the version with NULL embodied emissions or the wrong capacity: embodied CO2e of that node's pods becomes 0 and the node power envelope changes.

**Affected environments.** PostgreSQL for the float4 comparison; both databases for the missing transaction/constraint.

**Evidence.** Code read; PostgreSQL semantics checked read-only on the live database: `select (1037.3::real)::double precision` = 1037.300048828125. Live kind: 708 versions per node, alternating embodied NULL/100 every few seconds.

**Fix.** 1. Migration: `ALTER TABLE node_snapshots_scd ALTER COLUMN cpu_capacity_cores TYPE DOUBLE PRECISION, ALTER COLUMN embodied_emissions_kg TYPE DOUBLE PRECISION` (same for node_snapshots). 2. Compare with a tolerance for floats (`math.isclose(rel_tol=1e-6)`), and never treat NULL -> value of embodied_emissions_kg from a writer that does not know the value as a change: writers pass only the fields they own (node analysis must not null the embodied value; fix shared with NRG-07). 3. Run save_nodes in one transaction with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the current row, and add `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_node_scd_current ON node_snapshots_scd(node_name) WHERE is_current` (after a data fix that keeps the newest current row per node). 4. Data repair migration: collapse consecutive versions of a node that are equal on every compared column (keep the first valid_from and the last valid_to). 5. Stop writing the legacy node_snapshots table in SQLite or prune it with the raw retention.

**Acceptance.** PostgreSQL test: save a node with cpu_capacity_cores=3.92 and embodied_emissions_kg=1037.3 one hundred times: exactly one row. Two concurrent save_nodes calls with different values: exactly one current row per node, valid intervals contiguous. After the repair migration on a copy of the live kind database each node has at most a handful of versions.

#### STO-07 · No read path uses calculation_version or model_version, the hourly tables do not keep model_version, and rows written before the model changes (documented as about 2x inflated CPU and energy) are summed with current rows without any flag.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** design · **Confidence** confirmed-code · **Effort** M

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:132`; `src/greenkube/core/historical_range_processor.py:47-49`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0050_impact_allocation.sql:1-12`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:709-803`; `CHANGELOG.md:334`

**Mechanism.** Raw rows carry `calculation_version` (`<version>+range.3`) and `model_version` (Impact Model 2.0.0, NULL on rows written before migration 0050). `grep` over src/greenkube shows that the only readers are `greenkube recompute` (raw rows only) and the compressor, which stores `MAX(calculation_version)` (a lexical maximum, so `0.10.0+range.3` < `0.9.0+range.2`) and drops model_version: combined_metrics_hourly and container_metrics_hourly have no model_version column. No aggregate query (aggregate_summary, aggregate_timeseries, aggregate_by_namespace, aggregate_top_pods, summary refresher, reports) filters or reports by version. CHANGELOG states that rows stored before the A1 fix are inflated (pod cgroup summed with container series, about 2x CPU and energy) and that `older raw and hourly rows are not rewritten`. An install upgraded from 0.3.1 therefore shows monthly and yearly totals that add legacy-semantics hours to new ones, with nothing in the API or UI to tell them apart, and legacy hourly rows can never be recomputed (their raw rows are gone).

**Effect on numbers.** For an install upgraded after N months, the yearly energy and CO2e total contains N months counted about 2x: for 6 legacy months out of 12 the yearly total is about +50% too high; the dashboard shows a 50% drop at the upgrade date that looks like a saving. Legacy rows also have step-end timestamp semantics, NULL cost components and `intensity_source=unknown`.

**Affected environments.** Every install upgraded from 0.3.x (and every future Impact Model version bump).

**Evidence.** Code: grep of calculation_version/model_version (only writers, compressor, impact/recompute.py); live schema of combined_metrics_hourly has calculation_version but no model_version; CHANGELOG.md line 334.

**Fix.** 1. Migration: add `model_version TEXT` to both hourly tables; compressor stores the single version of the hour or 'mixed' (same rule as `_source_aggregate`), and replaces MAX(calculation_version) with the same rule. 2. Add a small table `metric_semantics_epochs(valid_from, calculation_version, model_version, note)` written when the controller first runs a new version, and return from every aggregate endpoint a `model_versions` list plus `legacy_share` (fraction of the energy that comes from rows whose model_version is NULL or older than the current one). The UI marks the legacy period on time series (annotation at the epoch boundary) and adds a warning on totals that span it. 3. Offer `greenkube recompute --hourly`: for legacy hourly rows apply the documented correction only when it is exact; otherwise let the operator exclude legacy periods (`exclude_legacy=true` query parameter, default true for savings and report endpoints). 4. Backfill: mark existing hourly rows with model_version NULL -> 'legacy' during the migration.

**Acceptance.** Database seeded with hours of calculation_version '0.3.1' and hours of the current version: `GET /api/v1/metrics/summary` over both returns legacy_share > 0 and both versions; with exclude_legacy=true the total equals the sum of current-version rows only; compression of an hour holding two versions stores 'mixed'; '0.10.0' is never ordered below '0.9.0'.

#### STO-08 · Unknown requests, cost and energy are stored as 0 (model and schema defaults) instead of NULL, so SUM, AVG and MAX treat missing measurements as real zeros and hourly rows erase the difference.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** design · **Confidence** confirmed-live · **Effort** L

**Where.** `src/greenkube/models/metrics.py:912-944`; `src/greenkube/core/historical_range_processor.py:808-809`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0033_container_metrics.sql:7-16`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0002_hourly_aggregation_and_node_scd2.sql:15-22`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:108-122`

**Mechanism.** CombinedMetric declares `total_cost: float = 0.0`, `co2e_grams = 0.0`, `joules = 0.0`, `cpu_request: int = 0`, `memory_request: int = 0`, `pue = 1.0`, `grid_intensity = 0.0`; the container and hourly tables add `DEFAULT 0` on the same columns. The processor writes `pod_request_map.get(pod_key, 0)`. A BestEffort pod (really no request) and a pod whose spec was not available at collection time are both stored as 0; a pod without cost data and a free pod are both 0 (only cost_source tells, and it is not used by the aggregates). The compressor then stores MAX(cpu_request), SUM(total_cost), AVG(pue), AVG(grid_intensity) over these zeros.

**Effect on numbers.** Live minikube: 11,210 of 44,105 pod rows (25%) have cpu_request=0; the same pod has both 0 and non-zero requests in different steps (greenkube-controller: 4 rows at 0, 22 at its real request; oversized-worker: 3 at 0, 17 real). Any AVG of the request over the window is biased low (22 x 500m and 4 x 0 gives 423m instead of 500m, -15%), reservation shares of energy and cost of these steps go to other pods, and AVG(grid_intensity) of an hour that mixes 0 (unknown) with 56 gives a meaningless value.

**Affected environments.** All.

**Evidence.** Live: `select cost_source, count(*), count(*) filter (where cpu_request=0) ... from combined_metrics group by 1` (custom_rate: 10821 of 11003 at request 0); `select pod_name, count(*) filter (where cpu_request=0), count(*) filter (where cpu_request>0) ... having both > 0`. Related collection-side findings: COL-01, COL-07, NRG-11, COST-05.

**Fix.** Make the measurement columns nullable end to end: CombinedMetric fields `Optional[...] = None` for cpu_request, memory_request, total_cost, joules, co2e_grams, grid_intensity; migrations drop the DEFAULT 0 on raw, container and hourly tables (no rewrite of existing rows; add `request_source TEXT` with values spec|unknown and backfill 'unknown' where cpu_request=0 AND memory_request=0 AND owner_kind IS NULL). The processor writes NULL when the pod spec is unknown and 0 only when the spec is known and has no request. Aggregates: SUM ignores NULL but every aggregate returns `coverage` = rows with a value / rows; the compressor stores MAX over non-NULL and a `request_known_samples` count; consumers (rightsizing, impact shares) must skip steps with NULL requests instead of using 0.

**Acceptance.** A window collected while the pod list fails stores cpu_request NULL and request_source=unknown; aggregate endpoints return coverage < 1 for it; the hourly row of an hour with 10 known steps at 500m and 2 unknown has cpu_request=500 and request_known_samples=10; a BestEffort pod has cpu_request=0 and request_source=spec.

#### STO-09 · Metric rows carry no cluster identity and nothing prevents two installs from writing the same database schema; keys are (pod_name, namespace, timestamp) and (node, timestamp).

**Severity** medium · **Kind** design · **Confidence** confirmed-code · **Effort** M

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0000_initial_schema.sql:3-41`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0050_impact_allocation.sql:13-37`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0038_collection_watermarks.sql:3-9`; `src/greenkube/core/collection.py:221`

**Mechanism.** collection_watermarks, the savings ledger and recommendations are keyed by a cluster name or uid, but combined_metrics, container_metrics, node_idle_metrics, both hourly tables and node_snapshots_scd have no cluster column. (1) Two GreenKube installs pointed at one external PostgreSQL with the default DB_SCHEMA (a plausible operator shortcut: one managed database for dev and prod clusters) write into the same tables: each keeps its own watermark, rows of same-named objects (kube-system static pods such as etcd-<node>, StatefulSet pods such as prometheus-k8s-0, nodes named node-1 or minikube) overwrite each other on every step, and all other rows are added together in every total. Nothing detects it. (2) When the cluster name changes (COST-04, COL-02) the rows cannot be attributed to the old or new identity, which is why the fork can only be handled by re-collecting. (3) A database restored into another cluster carries rows that look native.

**Effect on numbers.** Two clusters on one schema: every dashboard total is the sum of both clusters (about 2x for similar clusters) and colliding keys flip between the two clusters' values at each collection.

**Affected environments.** PostgreSQL shared by several installs; any cluster rename or database restore.

**Evidence.** Live schema and migrations: no cluster column in the metric tables; unique indexes combined_metrics(pod_name, namespace, timestamp), node_idle_metrics(node, timestamp).

**Fix.** 1. Add a singleton table `installation(cluster_uid TEXT PRIMARY KEY, cluster_name, created_at)`, written once by the controller from the kube-system namespace UID. At every start compare it with the live cluster UID and refuse to collect (clear error, `/readyz` not ready) when they differ, unless `ALLOW_CLUSTER_UID_CHANGE=true` is set once. 2. Key the watermark by that uid, not by the display name (fixes the fork at its root). 3. When multi-cluster becomes a goal (roadmap P7), add cluster_uid to the metric tables and to their unique keys; until then the guard of step 1 is enough. 4. Document `one schema per cluster` in docs/installation.md.

**Acceptance.** Starting a second controller against a database whose installation.cluster_uid differs fails before any write with an explicit message; renaming CLUSTER_NAME does not change the watermark key or trigger a re-collection.

#### STO-10 · PostgreSQL time buckets and report years are computed with date_trunc/EXTRACT in the session time zone, which the pool never sets to UTC, so on a database whose default time zone is not UTC daily, weekly, monthly and yearly figures are shifted and labelled with the wrong date.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** portability · **Confidence** confirmed-code · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/db.py:119-128`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:985-1004`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:855-856`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:677-696`

**Mechanism.** `asyncpg.create_pool(... server_settings={'search_path', 'statement_timeout'})` does not set `timezone`. `aggregate_timeseries` groups by `date_trunc('<unit>', ts)` on TIMESTAMPTZ values, `aggregate_grouped_row_count` uses `date_trunc('<unit>', timestamp)` and `list_metric_years` uses `EXTRACT(YEAR FROM timestamp)`. On timestamptz these functions work in the session TimeZone. The bucket comes back as an aware datetime in UTC and is formatted with a fixed pattern (`'%Y-%m-%dT00:00:00Z'`, `'%Y-%m-01T00:00:00Z'`, `'%Y-%m-%dT%H:00:00Z'`) that overwrites the hour and minute. The compressor (`_POSTGRES_HOUR`) and the SQLite queries use explicit UTC, so the write side and the SQLite dialect disagree with these reads. The bundled PostgreSQL runs in UTC; an external PostgreSQL (supported by the chart) with `timezone` set at server, database or role level does not.

**Effect on numbers.** Europe/Paris: the day bucket of 2026-11-15 starts at 2026-11-14T23:00Z and is labelled `2026-11-14T00:00:00Z`: every daily total is shown one day early and covers a window shifted by 1 to 2 hours. Asia/Kolkata: the hourly bucket of 10:00Z becomes 09:30Z, labelled `09:00:00Z`, and merges two half hours of different UTC hours. America/New_York: data of 2026-11-01 00:00-04:00Z is reported in October, and the first hours of 1 January in the previous year's report.

**Affected environments.** PostgreSQL whose default TimeZone is not UTC (typical for self-hosted on-prem databases; managed cloud databases are UTC unless changed). Not the bundled StatefulSet.

**Evidence.** Code; PostgreSQL semantics checked read-only on the live database with the explicit-zone form: `select date_trunc('day', timestamptz '2026-11-15 10:00+00', 'Europe/Paris') at time zone 'UTC'` = 2026-11-14 23:00:00; Kolkata hour = 2026-11-15 09:30:00; New York month of 2026-11-01 00:30Z = 2026-10-01 04:00:00. Not reproduced with a session whose TimeZone is changed (read-only rule).

**Fix.** 1. Add `'timezone': 'UTC'` to the pool `server_settings` in core/db.py and to cli/db.py's asyncpg.connect. 2. Make the SQL independent of the session anyway: replace `date_trunc(u, ts)` with `date_trunc(u, ts, 'UTC')` (PostgreSQL 12+) or `date_trunc(u, ts AT TIME ZONE 'UTC') AT TIME ZONE 'UTC'`, and `EXTRACT(YEAR FROM ts)` with `EXTRACT(YEAR FROM ts AT TIME ZONE 'UTC')`, in every repository (grep date_trunc and EXTRACT). 3. Format buckets with `isoformat()` of the returned value instead of a pattern that overwrites the time part, and assert the returned bucket is at 00:00 UTC for day and coarser units. 4. If local-time reporting is wanted, add an explicit REPORT_TIMEZONE setting and return offsets.

**Acceptance.** Integration test on PostgreSQL with `ALTER ROLE <user> SET timezone='Asia/Kolkata'`: rows at 2026-11-14T23:30Z and 2026-11-15T00:30Z give two daily buckets labelled 2026-11-14 and 2026-11-15, hourly buckets on whole UTC hours, and list_metric_years returns the UTC year; results equal the SQLite ones.

#### STO-11 · The PostgreSQL carbon recompute compares a float4 column with a float8 intensity (`IS DISTINCT FROM`), so every row whose intensity is not float4-exact is rewritten on every hourly run.

**Severity** low · **Kind** performance · **Confidence** likely · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:1642-1643`; `src/greenkube/cli/start.py:122-146`

**Mechanism.** `recompute_carbon_with_latest_intensities` updates rows `WHERE t.grid_intensity IS DISTINCT FROM f.intensity OR ...`. combined_metrics.grid_intensity and container_metrics.grid_intensity are REAL; carbon_intensity_history.carbon_intensity is DOUBLE PRECISION. PostgreSQL widens the REAL to a double for the comparison, so a stored 56.37 (56.369998931884766) is always distinct from 56.37. The job runs after every intensity refresh over the last 24 h, so for zones whose provider returns decimals (Wattnet, `float(value)`) every pod and container row of the last 24 h is rewritten every hour, and the log line `Recomputed N combined metrics with consolidated intensities` reports N = all rows. The docstring promise `Rows whose intensity and provenance are unchanged are left untouched` does not hold. Values are not changed, but the write volume is 24x the insert volume, tables and indexes bloat, and on a large cluster the single UPDATE can exceed DB_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS (30 s); it then fails as a whole and rows keep their provisional intensity.

**Effect on numbers.** 1,000 pods at 5-minute steps: 288,000 pod rows + container rows rewritten per hour instead of 0. If the statement times out, CO2e of the last 24 h stays on provisional (realtime) intensities: the error is the provisional-to-consolidated difference of the provider (a few percent).

**Affected environments.** PostgreSQL with an intensity provider that returns non-integer values. Not observable on the live installs (no intensity history: zone default only).

**Evidence.** Code; PostgreSQL semantics verified read-only on the live database: `select 56.37::real is distinct from 56.37::double precision` returns true, `56::real is distinct from 56::double precision` returns false.

**Fix.** Store energy, CO2e, cost, pue and grid_intensity as DOUBLE PRECISION in the raw, container and hourly tables (same migration as NRG-06). Until then compare with `t.grid_intensity IS DISTINCT FROM f.intensity::real`. Run the recompute per hour bucket (24 statements) so one statement stays far below the timeout, and log updated versus examined rows.

**Acceptance.** PostgreSQL test: rows with intensity 56.37 from history; first recompute updates them, a second run returns 0 and leaves xmin of the rows unchanged.

#### STO-12 · SQLite stores timestamps as ISO text with variable fractional precision, so range bounds that fall in the same second as a row select the wrong rows.

**Severity** low · **Kind** bug · **Confidence** confirmed-sim · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/utils/time.py:46-48`; `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/repository.py:460-462`; `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/repository.py:1196-1198`; `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/collection_watermark_repository.py:9-10`

**Mechanism.** `to_iso_z` uses `datetime.isoformat()`, which prints microseconds only when they are not zero. Rows are step-aligned (`2026-11-15T10:00:00Z`), bounds derived from `now` are not (`2026-11-15T10:00:00.500000Z`). In text order '.' (0x2E) sorts before 'Z' (0x5A), so `10:00:00.5Z < 10:00:00Z`. A lower bound inside the second of a row includes the row that is before it, an upper bound excludes the row that is before it. The compressor works around it with an extra bucket predicate (comment at metrics_compressor.py:181-183), the watermark repository with a fixed-width format; every other SQLite read and the node repositories (isoformat with +00:00) do not.

**Effect on numbers.** One step is wrongly added or dropped at a window edge when a bound shares its second with a step boundary: with 5-minute steps a 1-in-300 chance per bound for arbitrary times, systematic for jobs scheduled on the minute. On a 1-hour window one step is 8% of the total.

**Affected environments.** SQLite only (demo, local development, the SQLite test suite).

**Evidence.** Simulation audit-workspace/audit/work/storage-write/sim_storage.py SIM C: `read_combined_metrics(10:00:00.5, 10:05:00.5)` returned the 10:00:00 row and not the 10:05:00 row; `read_combined_metrics(10:00:00, 10:00:00.5)` returned nothing.

**Fix.** Make `to_iso_z` fixed width: `ensure_utc(value).strftime('%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S.%fZ')` is not compatible with stored rows, so instead truncate every SQLite bound to whole seconds with explicit semantics (`>= floor(start)` and `< ceil(end)`), or store epoch seconds (INTEGER) in new SQLite columns and migrate. Use one helper for all SQLite repositories, including the node repositories.

**Acceptance.** SIM C as a test returns the 10:05:00 row for the first query and the 10:00:00 row for the second, on SQLite and PostgreSQL alike.

#### STO-13 · pod_count in summaries is COUNT(DISTINCT pod_name) without the namespace, so same-named pods of different namespaces are counted once.

**Severity** low · **Kind** bug · **Confidence** confirmed-code · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:785`; `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/repository.py:762`; `frontend/src/routes/footprint/+page.svelte:180`

**Mechanism.** aggregate_summary computes `COUNT(DISTINCT pod_name) AS pod_count` over the union of raw and hourly rows. The pod identity everywhere else is (namespace, pod_name). StatefulSet pods (`postgres-0`, `redis-0`, `prometheus-k8s-0`) commonly exist in several namespaces. The figure is shown as `Active Pods` on the Footprint page and as unique_pods in the report, although it counts every pod that had a row in the period, not the active ones.

**Effect on numbers.** A cluster with 20 namespaces each running `postgres-0` shows 1 pod instead of 20 for them. No collision in the two live clusters (89/89 and 48/48).

**Affected environments.** All.

**Evidence.** Code; live check `select count(distinct pod_name), count(distinct (namespace, pod_name)) from combined_metrics`.

**Fix.** Use `COUNT(DISTINCT (namespace, pod_name))` on PostgreSQL and `COUNT(DISTINCT namespace || '/' || pod_name)` on SQLite; rename the UI label to `Pods seen in period`.

**Acceptance.** Two rows (ns a, pod db-0) and (ns b, pod db-0): pod_count = 2 on both dialects.

#### STO-14 · The hourly tables drop columns of the raw rows (ephemeral storage, grid_intensity_timestamp, model_version, period) and compression rounds differently per dialect.

**Severity** low · **Kind** data-integrity · **Confidence** confirmed-code · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0002_hourly_aggregation_and_node_scd2.sql:9-50`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:104-165`

**Mechanism.** combined_metrics_hourly has no ephemeral_storage_request_bytes, ephemeral_storage_usage_bytes, grid_intensity_timestamp or model_version column, and `estimation_reasons` is replaced by '[]'. After METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS these values are no longer readable and after the raw retention they are gone. `cpu_usage_avg` is `AVG(...)::INTEGER` on PostgreSQL (rounds) and `CAST(AVG(...) AS INTEGER)` on SQLite (truncates); owner, node and zone use MODE() on PostgreSQL and an arbitrary row on SQLite.

**Effect on numbers.** Ephemeral-storage history is limited to 24 h in API reads; a pod averaging 0.6 millicores is 1 on PostgreSQL and 0 on SQLite in hourly data.

**Affected environments.** All.

**Evidence.** Live `information_schema.columns` of combined_metrics_hourly versus combined_metrics; metrics_compressor.aggregate_columns.

**Fix.** Migration adding the missing columns to the hourly table (MAX for the two ephemeral columns, model_version per STO-07 rule, MIN(grid_intensity_timestamp)); use ROUND(AVG()) on both dialects and a deterministic mode (most frequent, ties by name) on SQLite.

**Acceptance.** Compress one hour on both dialects from the same raw rows: identical hourly rows, with ephemeral columns equal to the raw maxima.

#### STO-15 · All pending migrations run in one transaction that takes ACCESS EXCLUSIVE locks on the metric tables and then rewrites them (0039 backfill), with the statement timeout disabled.

**Severity** low · **Kind** reliability · **Confidence** likely · **Effort** M

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/migrations/runner.py:93-119`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0039_metric_provenance.sql:8-104`

**Mechanism.** `_run_postgres` opens one transaction for the whole batch (0038 to 0113 when upgrading from 0.3.1), sets `statement_timeout = 0` and applies every script. 0039 first runs `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN` on combined_metrics, combined_metrics_hourly, container_metrics and container_metrics_hourly (ACCESS EXCLUSIVE, held until the batch commits) and then `UPDATE` every row of the four tables, the container one with two correlated sub-selects per row. Every row is rewritten once (table size doubles until vacuum) and no reader or writer can touch the tables until all later migrations have finished. Old API pods still running during the rolling upgrade block and then fail on their 30 s statement timeout. A failure in any later script rolls the whole batch back and the controller repeats the full rewrite at each restart. The runner also keeps no checksum of applied scripts and applies any version missing from schema_migrations regardless of order (the shipped numbering has gaps: 0047-0049, 0051, 0062, 0067...), so a script added later with a lower number runs after higher ones on existing databases and before them on new ones, and an edited script is never noticed. (Scripts 0000-0037 are unchanged since v0.3.1.)

**Effect on numbers.** No number is changed. Availability: with 7 days of raw rows for 2,000 pods (about 4 million pod rows and more container rows) the dashboard and API are unavailable for the duration of the rewrite (minutes).

**Affected environments.** PostgreSQL upgrades from 0.3.x with a populated database.

**Evidence.** Code read (runner.py and 0039). Not timed on a large database.

**Fix.** Run one transaction per migration (keep the advisory lock for the batch), split data backfills from DDL into separate versions executed in batches by key range outside the DDL transaction, and record progress so a restart resumes. Document expected durations in docs/upgrading.md.

**Acceptance.** Upgrade test with 5 million seeded rows: no lock on combined_metrics is held for more than a few seconds (checked through pg_locks sampling), and killing the controller during the backfill resumes without redoing completed ranges.

#### STO-16 · `greenkube db copy` drops all metric history by default and the documentation says it is re-collected, although only the last COLLECTION_MAX_BACKFILL (24 h) can be.

**Severity** low · **Kind** docs · **Confidence** confirmed-code · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/cli/db.py:26-28`; `src/greenkube/cli/db.py:53-75`; `docs/upgrading.md:132-135`

**Mechanism.** The SQLite to PostgreSQL copy excludes METRIC_TABLES (raw, hourly and container metrics, idle rows, node snapshots, intensity history, usage rollups and both watermarks) unless `--include-metrics` is given. The docstring and docs/upgrading.md say `Metric history is large and is re-collected`. After the copy the controller has no watermark and collects only the last 24 h; hourly history of the previous months cannot be rebuilt (Prometheus retention is usually 15 days and the backfill horizon 24 h). The savings ledger, baselines and change measurements are copied and keep referring to periods for which the new database holds no metric.

**Effect on numbers.** Every dashboard, report and yearly total starts at migration time minus 24 h; earlier footprint and cost history is 0 while ledger savings of the same periods are still shown.

**Affected environments.** Installs migrating from SQLite (0.3.x Helm with config.db.type=sqlite) to PostgreSQL.

**Evidence.** Code and docs read; not executed.

**Fix.** Copy the hourly tables, node snapshots, intensity history and watermarks by default (they are small) and keep only the raw tables behind `--include-raw-metrics`; correct the docstring and docs/upgrading.md to state exactly what is lost; print the first and last hour copied.

**Acceptance.** Copy of a SQLite file holding 90 days of hourly rows without any flag: the PostgreSQL combined_metrics_hourly has the same row count and sums; aggregate_summary over the 90 days matches the source within float tolerance.

#### STO-17 · CPU usage is stored as integer millicores (and hourly averages are cast to INTEGER), so pods using less than 0.5 m are stored as exactly 0 while carrying energy and cost.

**Severity** low · **Kind** data-integrity · **Confidence** confirmed-live · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/historical_range_processor.py:688`; `src/greenkube/core/historical_range_processor.py:868`; `src/greenkube/models/metrics.py:948-950`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:104-105`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0000_initial_schema.sql:14`

**Mechanism.** `int(round(cores * 1000))` is applied before storage and the columns cpu_usage_millicores, cpu_usage_avg and cpu_usage_max are INTEGER in both dialects. Energy is allocated from the unrounded value, so a row can have cpu_usage_millicores = 0 and joules > 0. The hourly table rounds a second time (`AVG(...)::INTEGER`, truncation on SQLite).

**Effect on numbers.** Live minikube: 16,572 of 45,049 pod rows (36.8%) have cpu_usage_millicores = 0, of which 16,522 have joules > 0; 40.2% of the rows are 1 or 2 m, where rounding is a 25-50% relative error. Pods such as demo-web (784 rows, 2,561 J) have a stored CPU sum of 0. Utilisation ratios of small requests (10 m request, 0.4 m usage: 0% instead of 4%) and cluster CPU sums in time series are quantised; rightsizing of small containers cannot distinguish 0.1 m from 0.49 m.

**Affected environments.** All; visible on small or idle clusters (on-prem labs, kind, minikube, dev namespaces).

**Evidence.** Live: `select count(*) filter (where cpu_usage_millicores=0 and joules>0), count(*) filter (where cpu_usage_millicores=0), count(*) from combined_metrics` = 16522 / 16572 / 45049; pods with sum(cpu_usage_millicores)=0 over more than 700 rows.

**Fix.** Store CPU usage as DOUBLE PRECISION millicores (or integer microcores) in raw, container and hourly tables and in CombinedMetric (`Optional[float]`); keep the API field name and round only for display. Migration: ALTER COLUMN TYPE (values already stored stay integers).

**Acceptance.** A pod with a constant 0.3 m usage is stored with 0.3 (raw) and 0.3 (hourly average) on both dialects; SUM over 100 such pods returns 30 m.

#### STO-18 · Neither CombinedMetric nor the metric tables reject NaN, infinite or negative values, and a single NaN stored in a PostgreSQL float column turns every SUM that includes the row into NaN.

**Severity** low · **Kind** design · **Confidence** plausible · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/models/metrics.py:902-1034`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0000_initial_schema.sql:3-41`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0033_container_metrics.sql:2-27`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0050_impact_allocation.sql:13-37`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:160-215`

**Mechanism.** CombinedMetric has no validator: `CombinedMetric(joules=nan, total_cost=inf, co2e_grams=-5, cpu_request=-1)` is accepted. The metric tables have no CHECK constraint. Prometheus samples and OpenCost numbers are filtered with math.isfinite at the collectors (historical_range_processor.py:123, opencost_collector.py:33), but values computed afterwards (allocation shares, intensity records written by save_history from `record.get('carbonIntensity')`, recompute updates, `greenkube recompute`, the push API) reach the database unchecked. PostgreSQL stores NaN in REAL/DOUBLE columns and `SUM` over a set containing one NaN is NaN (and NaN sorts above every number in MAX and ORDER BY); SQLite silently stores NaN as NULL, so the two dialects differ. No failure path that produces NaN was found in this audit (live tables hold no NaN, infinite or negative value); this is a missing safeguard.

**Effect on numbers.** One NaN row makes total cost, energy and CO2e of every period containing it NaN (shown as null or NaN in the API and UI) until the row is repaired by hand; after compression the hourly row carries it forever.

**Affected environments.** PostgreSQL (NaN propagation); both for negative values.

**Evidence.** `CombinedMetric(...)` with nan/inf/negative values accepted (run with the repo interpreter); read-only live select `select sum(x), max(x) from (values (1::real),('NaN'::real),(2::real)) v(x)` returns NaN, NaN; live tables: 0 NaN, 0 negative, 0 infinite rows on both installs; grep finds no CHECK constraint in the metric migrations.

**Fix.** Add a pydantic model validator on CombinedMetric and NodeIdleMetric (finite, and >= 0 for cost, energy, CO2e, requests, usage, duration > 0) that raises; the collection job quarantines the row and counts it (`greenkube_collection_rows_rejected_total{reason}`). Add CHECK constraints (`joules >= 0 AND joules <> 'NaN'` etc.) with NOT VALID then VALIDATE in a later migration. Validate intensity records before save_history.

**Acceptance.** Writing a row with NaN joules raises before any SQL; the PostgreSQL tables reject a direct INSERT of NaN or a negative cost; the window is logged as partial with the rejected row counted.

### 5.6 Rollups, compression, retention

**Coverage.** Read in full: core/metrics_compressor.py, core/workload_usage.py, storage/workload_usage_sql.py, storage/base_workload_usage_repository.py, storage/sqlite/workload_usage_repository.py, core/summary_refresher.py, core/aggregator.py, impact/recompute.py, cli/recompute.py; read the relevant parts of storage/base_repository.py (smart readers), storage/postgres/repository.py (aggregate_summary, aggregate_timeseries, hourly readers, carbon recompute), storage/sqlite/repository.py (aggregate_summary), storage/postgres/timeseries_cache_repository.py, cli/start.py (scheduling of compression/summary/recompute), cli/db.py (table selection, sequences), core/collection.py (backfill planning), verify/series.py and verifier._coverage_seconds (consumers of hourly rows), api/routers/dashboard.py and metrics.py, Helm values for retention/storage. Ran three simulations with the real code on temporary SQLite databases (work/rollup-retention/sim1.py, sim2.py, sim3.py): conservation, idempotence, boundary readers, compressor lag, late rows, pruning, rollup from raw vs hourly, rollout hour in change measurement, percentile estimator, step change, aggregator weighting. Live: read-only SQL on both clusters (summary vs direct SQL, table sizes, time zone, idle totals); both clusters hold less than 24 h of data and 0 hourly rows, so compression itself could not be observed live. NOT covered: storage/postgres/workload_usage_repository.py beyond its hour expression (assumed symmetric with SQLite), the SQLite/Postgres summary repositories' upsert bodies, by-namespace/top-pods/page SQL bodies beyond their cutoff lines, the savings-ledger compression (other dimension), PostgreSQL execution of the compressor (no test DSN used; SQL read only), memory/performance of scans (BUG-12), core/optimization/verifier.py legacy path beyond _coverage_seconds, the frontend rendering of caches, crash-injection between compression and pruning (reasoned from code only).

**Earlier defects not fully fixed.**

- BUG-06 (partially_fixed): Container rows are now recomputed with pod rows; node_idle_metrics rows are not (ROL-07).
- BUG-29 (partially_fixed): Hourly readers keep sample_count (checked in sim2.py); container estimation flag not checked here. Churn-hour coverage bug is new (ROL-03).

#### ROL-01 · Every reader that combines raw and hourly tables splits at wall-clock now - METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS instead of the compression watermark, so data between the watermark and that cutoff is read from neither table.

**Severity** high · **Kind** bug · **Confidence** confirmed-sim · **Effort** M

**Where.** `src/greenkube/storage/base_repository.py:213-236`; `src/greenkube/storage/base_repository.py:313-343`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:709-790`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:836`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:934-978`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:1056`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:1132`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:1270`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:1337`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:1418`; `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/repository.py:692-745`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:329-340`

**Mechanism.** The compressor only fills *_hourly for closed hours below floor_hour(now_at_run - AGE) and records that in metrics_compression_watermarks. Readers ignore the watermark: read_combined_metrics_smart / read_container_metrics_smart and the SQL aggregations (aggregate_summary, aggregate_timeseries, aggregate_by_namespace, aggregate_top_pods, aggregate_grouped_row_count, count_*_smart, read_combined_metrics_page) compute `cutoff = now - timedelta(hours=cfg.METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS)` and read raw rows only for `timestamp >= cutoff - 1 min` and hourly rows only for `hour_bucket < cutoff`. (1) The hour H = floor_hour(cutoff) cannot be compressed yet (it needs H+1h <= floor_hour(now-AGE)), so raw rows in [H, cutoff-1min) are in neither branch: a hole of 0..59 minutes that moves as a sawtooth with the minute of the request. (2) `hour_bucket >= start_time` drops the whole first bucket when the window start is not hour-aligned (now-7d never is), although the raw rows of that partial hour exist. (3) The compressor runs once per hour, swallows its own exceptions (run() logs and continues) and stops whenever the controller is down or not leader; every hour between the watermark and the cutoff is then invisible to all readers although the raw rows are still stored. Nothing reports the gap.

**Effect on numbers.** Cost, CO2e, energy, embodied totals, time series, namespace and top-pod breakdowns, reports/exports, recommendation fallback contexts and change-measurement windows are UNDER-stated for every window longer than AGE (24 h). Simulation: 3 days of 5-min rows (872 samples, cost 1 each): aggregate_summary returns 870 (2 samples in the boundary hole); a window starting at now-2d17min returns 570 against 579 in raw (-1.6%); with the compressor 6 h behind, the 3-day total is 798 instead of 872 (-8.5%). In production the permanent error is up to 1 h at the boundary plus up to 1 h at the window start (about 1% of a 7-day total, 4% of a 48 h total, changing from minute to minute so totals are not reproducible), and grows without bound while compression is late or failing.

**Affected environments.** all (both databases, every install older than 24 h)

**Evidence.** audit-workspace/audit/work/rollup-retention/sim1.py steps D and E (real SQLiteCombinedMetricsRepository + MetricsCompressor on a temporary SQLite database). Output: 'aggregate_summary [T0, NOW): cost 870.0 ... truth cost 872.0', 'window now-2d17m: summary cost 570.0 raw truth 579.0', 'compressor 6h behind: summary cost 798.0 truth 872.0'. The Postgres functions use the same cutoff expression (read end to end). Not observable on the live clusters yet: both have less than 24 h of data and 0 hourly rows.

**Fix.** Make the compression watermark the single raw/hourly boundary. Add a helper (e.g. CombinedMetricsRepository.compression_boundary(table)) that reads metrics_compression_watermarks.compressed_until for combined_metrics / container_metrics (cache it for a few seconds; when no watermark exists, the boundary is -infinity, i.e. read everything from raw). In every reader listed above replace `cutoff` by that boundary B (hour-aligned by construction): hourly branch = hour_bucket >= floor_hour(start) AND hour_bucket < min(B, end) restricted to buckets fully inside the window; raw branch = timestamp >= max(start, B) AND timestamp < end; for the partial first hour (start not hour-aligned and start < B) either read the raw rows when they still exist (timestamp >= start AND timestamp < ceil_hour(start)) or prorate the hourly row by covered seconds, and document which. Remove the 1-minute tolerance. Because raw rows below B may already be pruned only below min(retention cutoff, B), the raw branch is always complete. Add an invariant metric greenkube_metrics_compression_lag_seconds (now - AGE - watermark) and surface it in /api/v1/health and loop health; make MetricsCompressor.run() report failures through report_loop_failure instead of only logging. No data migration is needed (stored rows are intact); metrics_summary / metrics_timeseries_cache are rebuilt on the next refresh.

**Acceptance.** 1) sim1.py scenario: 3 days of 5-minute rows, compressor run once: aggregate_summary(T0, now) total_cost == SUM(total_cost) of raw rows (872), for any minute-of-hour of `now`. 2) Window start not hour-aligned (now-2d17min): summary equals the raw sum over the same interval (579). 3) Move the watermark 6 h back and delete those hourly buckets: totals unchanged (872). 4) Property test: for random (start, end, now, watermark) the union of the two branches covers every raw row exactly once (compare with a full raw sum while raw is unpruned), on SQLite and PostgreSQL. 5) Health endpoint shows a compression lag > 2 h as degraded.

#### ROL-02 · Rows written or updated in an already compressed hour never reach combined_metrics_hourly / container_metrics_hourly and are deleted by raw retention; the collection-watermark guard described in the compressor is never wired.

**Severity** high · **Kind** data-integrity · **Confidence** confirmed-sim · **Effort** L

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:9-14`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:329-340`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:365-369`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:531-559`; `src/greenkube/cli/start.py:251`; `src/greenkube/core/collection.py:136-165`; `src/greenkube/core/config.py:265`; `src/greenkube/core/config.py:291`; `src/greenkube/storage/sqlite/workload_usage_repository.py:147-165`

**Mechanism.** Compression is strictly forward-only: `_compression_lower_bound` returns the stored watermark, so an hour below it is never re-read. The docstring promises that compression never passes the collection watermark, but production builds `MetricsCompressor(get_db_manager())` (cli/start.py:251; demo/runner.py:180 too) without `collection_watermark_provider`, so the only protection is the 24 h age. Rows land below the compression watermark when: (a) `greenkube start --last 7d` (documented backfill, CollectionJob.run(last=...)); (b) COLLECTION_MAX_BACKFILL is raised above METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS, or the age is lowered (the only check is a log warning about carbon; no validator links the two settings); (c) a catch-up after an outage is still running when the hourly job passes the hour it is filling; (d) `greenkube recompute` and any upsert that rewrites raw rows older than 24 h. Those rows stay in the raw tables, are ignored by every dashboard/API reader (which read *_hourly for anything older than 24 h), and `_prune_raw` deletes them after METRICS_RAW_RETENTION_DAYS without ever aggregating them. In the meantime workload_usage_hourly DOES pick them up (id high-water marks), so the recommendation rollup and the hourly tables disagree. A late row that lands in an hour whose raw rows were already pruned makes read_hour_inputs take the 'raw' branch with only the late rows and replace the complete rollup hour with a partial one.

**Effect on numbers.** Cost / energy / CO2e / usage of the affected hours are understated in all history older than 24 h, permanently after 7 days. Simulation: an already compressed hour holding cost 12 receives a backfilled pod (cost 120) and a re-collection of the existing pod (12 -> 60): raw says 180, combined_metrics_hourly stays at 12, workload_usage_hourly says 180; 8 days later the raw rows are pruned and 168 of 1040 cost units (16%) are gone for good. A `greenkube start --last 7d` on a cluster whose collector was down therefore backfills 6 days that never appear in dashboards and disappear after a week.

**Affected environments.** all; triggered by --last backfills, COLLECTION_MAX_BACKFILL > METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS, lowered compression age (the accelerated e2e profile), long catch-ups, recompute

**Evidence.** audit-workspace/audit/work/rollup-retention/sim1.py steps F and H: 'late rows: raw cost of hour now 180.0', 'hourly cost of that hour after compression run: (12.0, 1)', 'workload_usage_hourly for that hour: [(late-pod, 120.0, raw), (p, 60.0, raw)]', 'permanently lost cost: 168.0'. grep shows collection_watermark_provider is referenced only inside metrics_compressor.py.

**Fix.** 1) Track dirty hours: reuse the rollup's mechanism (max(id) high-water marks) plus an `updated_at`/xmin-independent marker for in-place updates (add a monotonically increasing `write_seq` column filled from a sequence on INSERT and on ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE in write_combined_metrics / write_container_metrics, both dialects). On each run, before advancing, list the distinct hours below the watermark that received rows with write_seq above the saved mark and re-aggregate each of them from raw with the existing upsert, but only when the raw set of that hour is still complete (hour >= raw prune cutoff); otherwise merge additively (SUM columns added, sample_count added, max columns max'ed) or reject the write upstream. 2) Wire the guard: pass a provider returning the metrics collection watermark (collection_watermarks.last_complete_end) to MetricsCompressor in cli/start.py and demo/runner.py, and keep hours raw while a `--last` run is active (lease/flag row). 3) Add a Config validator: COLLECTION_MAX_BACKFILL <= METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS and METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS >= 1 (or >= safety lag + one scheduler interval); document it in docs/configuration.md and Helm values. 4) _prune_raw must refuse to delete an hour that is dirty (received writes after its compression). 5) Make read_hour_inputs choose 'raw' only when the hour is at or above the compression watermark or the raw row count equals the hourly sample_count; else combine. 6) Backfill for existing installs: one migration-time job that re-aggregates every hour still present in raw (last 7 days) so current divergences are repaired; older losses cannot be recovered and should be listed in the release notes.

**Acceptance.** 1) sim1.py step F: after writing 12 late rows for a new pod and re-writing 12 rows of an existing pod into a compressed hour, the next compressor.run() makes SUM(total_cost) of that hour in combined_metrics_hourly equal 180 and equal to workload_usage_hourly. 2) After advancing the clock 8 days, SUM over combined_metrics_hourly equals the sum of everything ever written (1040). 3) With a collection watermark provider returning T, no hour ending after T is compressed. 4) Config(COLLECTION_MAX_BACKFILL='72h', METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS=24) fails validation with a clear message. 5) Same tests on PostgreSQL where ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE keeps the row id.

#### ROL-03 · Change measurements built from compressed hourly rows overstate cost rate, replicas and core-hours for every hour in which the workload's pods were replaced, so the same window gives different numbers before and after compression.

**Severity** high · **Kind** bug · **Confidence** confirmed-sim · **Effort** L

**Where.** `src/greenkube/verify/series.py:120-126`; `src/greenkube/verify/series.py:160-186`; `src/greenkube/core/optimization/verifier.py:253-264`; `src/greenkube/verify/service.py:238-255`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:126`

**Mechanism.** hourly_buckets groups rows per timestamp and takes `coverage = max(_coverage_seconds(row) for row in members)` as the time covered by that timestamp group, then divides cost/CO2e by the covered seconds (`cost = acc.cost / acc.cost_seconds * 3600`) and computes replicas as pod_seconds / covered. For raw rows a group is one step, which is correct. After compression there is one row per pod and hour, all with timestamp = hour_bucket and duration_seconds = SUM of its samples. When no pod lived the whole hour (rollout, node drain, eviction, restart under a new name, CronJob), the group's coverage is the longest-lived pod's duration, not the union of the pods' lifetimes, because the hourly row no longer knows which steps the pod covered. The docstring claim 'raw 5-minute rows and hourly rollups mix correctly' is therefore false for churn hours. Pre-windows (7 d before the change) are read at baseline time and post-windows at decision time through read_combined_metrics_smart, so about 6/7 of every window comes from hourly rows.

**Effect on numbers.** Simulation: Deployment `web`, one replica, rollout at minute 20 (pod web-a 4 samples, web-b 8 samples), cost 1 per 5-minute sample (true 12 per hour, 1 replica, 0.5 core-hours). From raw rows: cost/h 12.0, replicas 1.0, core_hours 0.5. From the compressed rows of the same hour: cost/h 18.0 (+50%), replicas 1.5, core_hours 0.75, coverage 0.67. A 50/50 split gives +100%; three pod generations in one hour +200%. Workloads deployed daily get one inflated hour per day in pre and post windows (a few % bias on both baselines, extra variance in the bootstrap CI, and a different verdict depending on whether the hour was read before or after it turned 24 h old). Savings amounts written to the ledger from those series are therefore not reproducible from stored data.

**Affected environments.** all; every workload that is redeployed, rescheduled or autoscaled during a pre/post window (i.e. most GitOps-managed workloads)

**Evidence.** audit-workspace/audit/work/rollup-retention/sim2.py (real compressor + read_combined_metrics_smart + greenkube.verify.series.hourly_buckets): 'RAW 10:00 coverage 1.0 replicas 1.0 cost/h 12.0 core_hours 0.5' vs 'HOURLY 10:00 coverage 0.667 replicas 1.5 cost/h 18.0 core_hours 0.75'.

**Fix.** Preferred: stop deriving time coverage from per-pod hourly rows. (a) Read measurement windows from workload_usage_hourly, which already stores per target and hour `step_count` (distinct covered steps), `duration_seconds` (pod-seconds), `pod_count`, cost and CO2e: covered seconds = step_count x step (store `covered_seconds` explicitly in the rollup so that a later step change cannot corrupt it), replicas = duration_seconds / covered_seconds, cost rate = total_cost / covered_seconds x 3600. Add the trust columns the measurement needs (trusted cost seconds, trusted carbon seconds, usage source) to the rollup, or (b) add to combined_metrics_hourly the columns first_sample_at / last_sample_at and, per (workload, hour), persist `covered_seconds` in a small workload-hour table written by the compressor from raw rows (COUNT(DISTINCT timestamp) x step per owner). Then hourly_buckets must use that covered time for hourly groups instead of max(duration). Until then, mark hours whose hourly group has max(duration_seconds) < 3600 and more than one pod as `coverage unknown` and exclude them rather than inflating them. Backfill: workload_usage_hourly already has step_count for the retained lookback; older compressed hours cannot be corrected and must be treated as low-confidence (exclude from measurement). Update docs/methodology/savings-measurement.md.

**Acceptance.** 1) sim2.py scenario: hourly_buckets (or its replacement) returns cost/h 12.0, replicas 1.0, core_hours 0.5 for the rollout hour both before and after compression. 2) Property test: for random pod lifetimes inside an hour, the bucket computed from compressed data equals the bucket computed from the raw rows (cost, replicas, core_hours, coverage) within 1e-9. 3) A measurement re-run after its window has been compressed yields the same estimate and verdict as before compression (regression test on a temporary database).

#### ROL-04 · Lookback usage percentiles used for rightsizing are nearest-rank percentiles of hourly percentiles, and with a 5-minute step an hourly p95/p99 is the hourly maximum, so p95 is overstated whenever short peaks occur in more than 5% of the hours.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** design · **Confidence** confirmed-sim · **Effort** L

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/workload_usage.py:62-91`; `src/greenkube/core/workload_usage.py:324-334`; `src/greenkube/core/workload_usage.py:638-667`; `src/greenkube/storage/workload_usage_sql.py:198-287`

**Mechanism.** rollup_rows stores per hour weighted nearest-rank percentiles of the points of that hour. With one replica and a 5-minute step an hour has 12 points: nearest_rank_index(12, 95) = 11 and (12, 99) = 11, i.e. hourly p95 = hourly p99 = hourly max (p90 = second highest). summarize()/summary_sql then take the nearest-rank p-th value among the hourly p-values. The result is not the p-th percentile of the samples: one 5-minute peak per hour in more than 5% of the hours makes the lookback 'p95' equal to the peak, although the peak may be 1-3% of the samples. The value is exposed as cpu/memory p50..p99 on BaselineMetric and sizes requests.

**Effect on numbers.** Simulation: 14 days, one pod, 100 m baseline, one 5-minute spike to 900 m every 3 hours (2.8% of samples). True sample percentiles p50/p90/p95/p99 = 100/100/100/900; rollup lookback = 100/100/900/900. p95 is 9x too high, so a CPU request sized on p95 is recommended at about 900 m instead of about 100 m plus headroom: the recommended saving is understated (direction: conservative, but the evidence shown to the user is labelled p95 and is not). With many replicas or a 30 s step the hourly percentile is better resolved and the bias shrinks.

**Affected environments.** all; strongest with PROMETHEUS_QUERY_RANGE_STEP >= 5m (default) and workloads with one or two replicas

**Evidence.** audit-workspace/audit/work/rollup-retention/sim3.py part (a) (pure functions rollup_rows + summarize): 'true sample percentiles: [100, 100, 100, 900]' / 'rollup lookback: [100.0, 100.0, 900.0, 900.0]'.

**Fix.** Store a mergeable distribution per hour instead of four scalar percentiles: e.g. a fixed log-spaced histogram (64-128 buckets, relative error <= 2-3%) or a t-digest/DDSketch serialized in one column per resource in workload_usage_hourly (cpu_hist, memory_hist), built from the raw points of the hour (per replica, warm-up points excluded as today). Lookback percentiles are then computed from the sum of the hourly histograms (in Python after a bounded read of one row per target-hour, or with an aggregate over the bucket arrays in SQL), which equals the sample percentile up to the bucket width. Keep cpu_usage_max as is. Backfill: re-roll the hours still present in raw (7 days); for older rollup hours fall back to the current hourly-percentile rule and mark the summary as `percentile_method=hourly_rank` in the recommendation evidence until the lookback is fully covered by histograms. Update docs (rightsizing methodology) to state the estimator and its error bound.

**Acceptance.** 1) sim3.py (a): lookback p95 == 100 (+/- one histogram bucket) and p99 == 900. 2) Property test: for random series (1-20 replicas, steps 30 s / 5 m, 14 days) the lookback p50/p90/p95/p99 differ from numpy nearest-rank percentiles of all samples by at most the documented relative error. 3) SQL and Python reference implementations return identical values on SQLite and PostgreSQL.

#### ROL-05 · Compressed hours read back with a larger PROMETHEUS_QUERY_RANGE_STEP are counted as covering sample_count x new step, dividing measured cost and CO2e rates by the step ratio.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** bug · **Confidence** confirmed-sim · **Effort** S

**Where.** `src/greenkube/core/optimization/verifier.py:253-264`; `src/greenkube/verify/series.py:124-127`; `src/greenkube/core/workload_usage.py:251-259`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:493-499`

**Mechanism.** _coverage_seconds(row, step) returns max(sample_count * step, duration_seconds) with the step of the CURRENT configuration. A row collected at 1 m (duration_seconds 60, or an hourly row with sample_count 60 and duration 3600) read after the step was raised to 5 m gets coverage 300 s (or 18000 s): the stored duration, which is exact, is overridden by the product. Rates divide by that coverage. The rollup has the same family of problem in milder form: averages and percentiles are weighted by sample_count, so after a step change hours collected at the smaller step weigh proportionally more in lookback averages for up to RECOMMENDATION_LOOKBACK_DAYS.

**Effect on numbers.** Simulation: one hour of 1-minute rows, true cost 12 per hour. Read with step 60 s: cost/h 12.0. Read with step 300 s: cost/h 2.4 (5x too low), replicas and core_hours unchanged. A change whose post-window contains rows collected before a 30s/1m -> 5m step change gets a post cost 5-10x too low for those hours, i.e. inflated savings; a pre-window is unaffected only because its snapshot was computed before the change.

**Affected environments.** installs that raise PROMETHEUS_QUERY_RANGE_STEP (e.g. from the accelerated/e2e 30 s or 1 m profile to the 5 m default) while changes are being measured or history is re-read

**Evidence.** audit-workspace/audit/work/rollup-retention/sim3.py part (b): '1m rows read with step=60s: cost/h 12.0' / '1m rows read with step=300s: cost/h 2.4'.

**Fix.** Make stored duration authoritative: in _coverage_seconds use duration_seconds when it is present and > 0, and fall back to sample_count * step only when it is missing (legacy rows). Persist the collection step on each raw row and on hourly rows (or derive covered seconds as in ROL-03) so that no reader needs the current configuration to interpret history. In the rollup, weight averages by duration_seconds (time) instead of sample_count, and store the step (or covered seconds) per rollup row. Record the step in collection_watermarks and log/emit an event when it changes. No backfill needed: duration_seconds is already stored on rows.

**Acceptance.** 1) sim3.py (b): cost/h is 12.0 for both step values. 2) Legacy rows without duration_seconds still use sample_count * step. 3) Rollup test: 7 days at 5 m followed by 7 days at 1 m with usage 100 m then 900 m gives a lookback cpu avg of 500 m (time-weighted), not 767 m.

#### ROL-06 · `greenkube recompute` rewrites raw rows only: hourly aggregates and (on PostgreSQL) the usage rollup keep the old model's numbers, the recomputed values vanish when raw rows are pruned, and hourly tables have no model_version column to tell versions apart.

**Severity** medium · **Kind** design · **Confidence** confirmed-code · **Effort** M

**Where.** `src/greenkube/impact/recompute.py:12-15`; `src/greenkube/impact/recompute.py:153-162`; `src/greenkube/impact/recompute.py:269-273`; `src/greenkube/core/metrics_compressor.py:131-132`; `src/greenkube/core/migrations/scripts/postgres/0050_impact_allocation.sql:9-13`; `src/greenkube/storage/postgres/repository.py:306-316`; `src/greenkube/core/workload_usage.py:849-853`

**Mechanism.** MetricRecomputer re-allocates raw pod, container and idle rows hour by hour and upserts them. (1) Hours older than METRICS_COMPRESSION_AGE_HOURS are already compressed and are never re-compressed (ROL-02), and every dashboard reader takes those hours from the hourly tables: `greenkube recompute --since 7d` changes what the UI shows for the last 24 h only. (2) On PostgreSQL the upsert is ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE, the row id does not change, so WorkloadUsageRollup.hours_written_between (id ranges) does not see the hours and workload_usage_hourly keeps old cost/CO2e outside its 24 h refresh window. (3) After 7 days the recomputed raw rows are pruned and only the old-model aggregates remain. (4) model_version exists on combined_metrics, container_metrics and node_idle_metrics only; the hourly tables keep `calculation_version = MAX(calculation_version)` (a lexical max of a different field) and estimation_reasons = '[]', so a history that mixes Impact Model versions cannot be detected or filtered. The limitation is stated in the module docstring but not in the CLI output or the UI.

**Effect on numbers.** After an Impact Model version bump, history older than 24 h keeps the old allocation while new hours use the new one: any 7 d / 30 d / 1 y total and any pre/post comparison straddling the upgrade mixes two models with no flag (step change in cost/energy/CO2e equal to the model difference). Recompute reports 'recomputed N rows' for 7 days although only 1/7 of them is ever read by dashboards.

**Affected environments.** all; PostgreSQL additionally leaves workload_usage_hourly stale

**Evidence.** Code path read end to end: recompute.py writes via write_combined_metrics/write_idle_metrics only; compressor never re-reads hours below its watermark (confirmed by sim1.py step F); migration 0050 adds model_version to raw tables only (live: information_schema shows model_version in combined_metrics and not in combined_metrics_hourly).

**Fix.** 1) After a non-dry-run recompute, re-aggregate every touched hour that is below the compression watermark from its (still complete) raw rows into combined_metrics_hourly / container_metrics_hourly and re-roll workload_usage_hourly for those hours (call the dirty-hour path of ROL-02; pass the touched hours explicitly rather than relying on ids).
